By leveraging a preprocessing model, our approach mounts the challenges posed by data sparsity and sensor heterogeneity limitations. Liquor-HGNN ou
p 3 odel is to predict the edge-labels for leaky or non-leaky pipes at each time step based on the discrete spatio-temporal graph snapshot to localize anomalies. The specific objectives and quantifiable criteria for the success of the Liquor-HGNN approach are to localize leaks for all pipe sections (corresponding to edges of a graph) using measurement information from only around 10 percent of the nodes, to accurately forecast the start time of leaks and to propose an imputation method for handling missing data through prior forecasts. 1.4. For example, in the case of a faulty sensor, the anomaly eﬀect remains lo- calized to a single measurement point, whereas a leak induces consis- tent changes across multiple sensors due to the strong connectivity of the network. (12)) features, respectively:(11)̃𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)(11-a)𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖=𝑄𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝑐(𝑙)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡ℎ⁡(𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)√𝐷(11-b)̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=exp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡ℎ𝑣𝑖)(12)̃𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗·𝑉𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)(12-a)𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖=𝑄𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝑐(𝑙)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗)·𝐾𝐹⁡𝑡⁡(𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)√𝐷(12-b)̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅̅𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡(𝑙−1)𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗=exp⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)∑𝑣𝑖∈𝒱𝑒⁢𝑥⁢𝑝⁡(𝐴⁢𝐹⁡𝑡𝑣𝑖)\n\n### 4.2. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This information provides a crucial basis for subsequent analysis.\n\nImage: Fig 5\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (827KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 5. Analysis of the number of the recurrent layers: (a) shortest path from sensor nodes to non-sensor nodes; (b) MAE distribution corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers; (c) MAPE corresponding to different numbers of the recurrent layers.\n\nExperimental results (Figs. 5(b) and 5(c)) indicate that the best performance is not achieved at the maximum iteration of 11, but at 3 iterations of recurrent layers. This finding demonstrates that more iterations are not necessarily better; instead, they need to match the majority of the shortest path between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. When the number of iterations exceeds a certain threshold (such as 1 in this case), further increasing the number of recurrent layers has limited improvement on the model’s prediction performance. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\n\nFurther analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\n\nIn summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. It also effectively reduces the iteration of recurrent layers, greatly decreasing training time of the model. This advantage is crucial for predictive modeling of actual WDSs, especially for medium- and large-scale systems, where its efficiency will provide significant application value.\n\n#### 4.2.2. Hidden semantic analysis of gating mechanisms\n\nThrough an in-depth analysis of the weight matrices obtained from the trained reset gate (Eq. (6)) and the update gate (Eq. (7)), this study reveals the unique mechanism by which the model selectively retains short- and long-term information between nodes. Analysis of the model parameters reveals that the weights of the two critical gates consistently remain close to 0.5. The balance between the reset gate and update gate indicates that short- and long-term memory are equally important in the forecasting model. This finding inspires a new perspective on model simplification: replacing complex gating mechanisms with several constants. This operation will significantly reduce the model’s parameters, thereby enhancing its performance and facilitating its application in larger-scale WDSs.\n\nThe CHPS (Eq. (8)) demonstrates how the AGN model efficiently retains key information. By visually mapping the attention weight of the hidden state layer onto the network (Fig. 6), we can observe how sensor nodes influence non-sensor nodes and how the model selectively focuses on feature correlation. For visualization purposes, only edges with weights greater than 0.01 are displayed in the figure, and the weights of nodes 215 were divided by 10. This analysis deepens our understanding of the model’s internal hidden mechanisms and highlights the impact of sensor layout on prediction performance.\n\nImage: Fig 6\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (2MB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 6. Attention weight of the CHPS.\n\nThe study found significant differences in the contributions of various sensor nodes during the prediction process, reflecting their interdependence and the strategic significance of sensor layout. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\n\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nFrontiers | Dynamic balancing method of power distribution and consumption tasks based on state iterative prediction and resource peaking shifting (https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/energy-research/articles/10.3389/fenrg.2024.1398647/full)\nciteturn65search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.5 years ago; Crawled: 2 months ago; ### 3.2 Resource demand states prediction model of power distribution and consumption tasks ... Explainable Graph Neural Network for Real-time Demand States Forecasting in Water Distribution System\nHowever, existing models often accumulate long-term prediction errors and fail to capture the strong temporal dependencies in measured time series. To address these challenges, this study proposes the Kolmogorov–Arnold Attention Network for the real-time modeling of WDSs (KANSA), which combines Kolmogorov–Arnold Networks with attention mechanisms to extract temporal dependency features through bidirectional spatiotemporal processing. Additionally, a multi-equation soft-constraint formulation embeds mass and energy conservation laws into the loss function, mitigating cumulative errors and enhancing physical consistency. Evaluations on a benchmark network and a real-world system demonstrate that KANSA achieves high-accuracy real-time estimation and pattern fidelity while maintaining engineering-grade hydraulic balance.\n\n[Button: Read more]\n\nDownload\n\n* * *\n\nExplainable Graph Neural Network for Real-time Demand States Forecasting in Water Distribution System\n\n  * Article\n\nAugust 2025\n\n·\n\n20 Reads\n\n·\n\n3 Citations\n\nResults in Engineering\n\nZekun Zou\n\n·\n\nZhihong Long\n\n·\n\nZipeng Zhu\n\n·\n\n[...]\n\n·\n\nTingchao Yu\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nTABLE 1. By leveraging a preprocessing model, our approach mounts the challenges posed by data sparsity and sensor heterogeneity limitations. Liquor-HGNN ou\\r\\np 3 odel is to predict the edge-labels for leaky or non-leaky pipes at each time step based on the discrete spatio-temporal graph snapshot to localize anomalies. The specific objectives and quantifiable criteria for the success of the Liquor-HGNN approach are to localize leaks for all pipe sections (corresponding to edges of a graph) using measurement information from only around 10 percent of the nodes, to accurately forecast the start time of leaks and to propose an imputation method for handling missing data through prior forecasts. 1.4. Method To address the complex task of leakage detection, we propose the Liquor-HGNN model1. The model selects the most suitable threshold based the Economic Score Metric, that is built upon the metric of KIOS research center [6]. The Economic Score function iterates over the d\\r\\np 4 e procedure. Barros et al. [27] introduce a signal processing approach. None of these approaches modeled the task using graph neural networks except for Gardars- son et al. [28], who trained two Chebyshev polynomial kernel Graph Convolutional Networks. Although they stated to gain a better economic score, their results have not been reproducable according to the delivered code and have thus been excluded from the conducted experiments. On the other hand, there is an increasing number of papers that use Heterogeneous Graph Neural Networks (HGNN) for link prediction like HetGNN [29] or MTHetGNN [30]. 3. Dataset Description The BattLeDIM 2020 dataset [6] was created using a real water distribution network in Cyprus, covering a pipe length of 42.6 km. It contains information on pipe breaks, water losses, and two \\r\\np 5 Graph Neural Network. All measurements for five-minute timesteps for 80 percent of the year 2018 are considered as training data, 20 percent as validation data and the full year of 2019 as test dataset. Each node has a unique demand pattern for each consumer type, based on the statistical characteristics. Additionally, 82 Automated Metered Readings (AMRs) offer aggregated demand data (see Appendix). The dataset includes the physical network structure and coordinates of the pipes, making it suitable for water network simulations with the Water Network Tool for Resilience (WNTR), a Python package which supports pressure-driven demand simulations and leakage modelling [7]. We then compute the heterogeneous graph convolution by using the GAT- Conv [32] at a given time 𝑡. Results The following section presents the results of benchmarking experiments conducted on the BattleDIM dataset, comparing against other submissions in the BattleDIM 2020 challenge. Classiﬁcation and pre-localization of anomalies Although leaks and pipe bursts represent the most common anoma- lies in WDNs (and pose the greatest risk to infrastructure integrity), they are not the only anomaly type. Other possible events include (Jian et al., 2022): ∙ blockages and deposits; ∙ pump or valve failures (e.g., pumps operating outside schedule, stuck gate valves, malfunctioning control valves); ∙ sensor malfunctions; ∙ unauthori\\r\\np 8 cally manifest as one-sided changes, such as a systematic pres- sure drop. More generally, the de- velopment of classiﬁcation rules requires the identiﬁcation of discrim- inative features that distinguish one anomaly type from another. For example, in the case of a faulty sensor, the anomaly eﬀect remains lo- calized to a single measurement point, whereas a leak induces consis- tent changes across multiple sensors due to the strong connectivity of the network. {p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}\\end{array}\\right]\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I\\times J} at I vertices of historical J time steps, nodal heights F{h}_{{v}_{I}}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I} and nodal types F{t}_{{v}_{I}}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I} at I vertices, the training task is to forecast next {J}^{*} time steps demand, denoted as {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}=\\left[\\begin{array}{ccc}{h}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {h}_{{v}_{1},{t}_{J*}}\\\\ ⋮& \\ddots & ⋮\\\\ {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{1}}& \\cdots & {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}\\end{array}\\right]\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{I\\times {J}^{*}}, that achieves trustworthy approximations of the ground truths {\\widehat{h}}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}, which is given by:(3)Training\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I}}\\right)=\\underset{\\Theta }{argmin}\\frac{1}{{J}^{*}}\\sum _{J}^{J+{J}^{*}}\\left|{h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}-{\\widehat{h}}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J*}}\\right|where \\Theta denotes all learnable parameters in the AGN model. It is crucial to emphasize that assuming inputs from pressure sensors and calculating losses based on all nodes is impractical in real situations. That is, nodes to be generalized (i.e. non-sensor nodes) cannot be considered known during training to prevent information leakage [19,33]. Thus, despite the aim of this study is to achieve prediction of all nodes, the input dimension of {h}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}} in Eq. (3) is I\\times J, instead of \\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times J.\n\nThe training challenge lies in learning the transferable correlation between sensor measurements and nodal features. After acquiring the trained model of the I sensor nodes, forecasting all I+{I}^{*} nodes are accomplished by additionally inputting the F{h}_{{v}_{{I}^{*}}} and F{t}_{{v}_{{I}^{*}}} of the remaining {I}^{*} non-sensor nodes:(4)Generalizing\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}\\right)={f}_{\\Theta }\\left({p}_{{v}_{I},{t}_{J}}+F{h}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}+F{t}_{{v}_{I+{I}^{*}}}\\right)={h}_{{v}_{I+I*},{t}_{J*}}\n\nThe update gate weight {u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)} represents the mixing ratio of past and current information. If the update gate value approaches 0, the CHPS {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)} will be mainly determined by the past CHPS {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}. Conversely, if the update gate weight approaches 1, the CHPS will be mainly determined by the current CHPS {\\tilde{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}, and the model will focus more on the current input {p}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}:(9){c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)}=\\left(1-{u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\right)\\odot {\\overline{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}+{u}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\\odot {\\tilde{c}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}\n\n(3) Hidden pressure feature transfer based on recurrence: By looping the steps described in Eq. (6) to Eq. (9), the learned CHPSs of sensor nodes are progressively generalized to non-sensor nodes through the weighted adjacency matrix.\n\n(4) Integration of nodal type and height features: By calculating the external-attention of the CHPSs to the nodal height and nodal type features, the potential nodal features can be obtained. These features are then fused with the CHPSs using addition and multiplication, respectively, to forecast the future nodal heads:(10){h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)}=\\left({c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)}+{\\tilde{Fh}}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)\\odot {\\tilde{Ft}}_{{v}_{i}}where potential height feature {\\tilde{Fh}}_{{v}_{i}} and the potential type feature {\\tilde{Ft}}_{{v}_{i}} are obtained by calculating the external-attention of CHPS {c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)} to nodal height F{h}_{{v}_{i}} (Eq. (11)) and nodal type F{t}_{{v}_{i}} (Eq. (12)) features, respectively:(11){\\tilde{Fh}}_{{v}_{i}}={\\stackrel{‾}{AFh}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}·{V}_{Fh}\\left(F{h}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)(11-a)AF{h}_{{v}_{i}}=\\frac{{Q}_{Fh}\\left({c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)}\\right)·{K}_{Fh}\\left(F{h}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}{\\sqrt{D}}(11-b){\\stackrel{‾}{AFh}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{\\mathrm{exp}\\left(AF{h}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}{{\\sum }_{{v}_{i}\\in \\mathcal{V}}exp\\left(AF{h}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}(12){\\tilde{Ft}}_{{v}_{i}}={\\stackrel{‾}{AFt}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}·{V}_{Ft}\\left(F{t}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)(12-a)AF{t}_{{v}_{i}}=\\frac{{Q}_{Ft}\\left({c}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l\\right)}\\right)·{K}_{Ft}\\left(F{t}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}{\\sqrt{D}}(12-b){\\stackrel{‾}{AFt}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}^{\\left(l-1\\right)}=\\frac{\\mathrm{exp}\\left(AF{t}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}{{\\sum }_{{v}_{i}\\in \\mathcal{V}}exp\\left(AF{t}_{{v}_{i}}\\right)}\n\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\n\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\n\n### 3.2. Model settings\n\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\n\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\n\n### 3.3. Evaluation metric\n\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:(13)MAE=\\frac{1}{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times {J}^{*}}\\sum _{i=1}^{I+{I}^{*}}\\sum _{j=1}^{{J}^{*}}\\left|{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}-{\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}\\right|(14)MAPE=\\frac{1}{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times {J}^{*}}\\sum _{i=1}^{I+{I}^{*}}\\sum _{j=1}^{{J}^{*}}\\frac{\\left|{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}-{\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}\\right|}{{h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}}}where {h}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}} is the prediction and {\\tilde{h}}_{{v}_{i},{t}_{j}} is the simulated measurement of vector {v}_{i} at time step {t}_{j}.\n\n### 4.2. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. ## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\nA case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.\n\n  * ### Enhanced Coordinated Sensor Network for Real-Time Lake Kivu Water Quality Monitoring\n\n2026, International Journal of Safety and Security Engineering\n\n  * ### A physics-informed AI methodology for Prognostics and Health Management of complex systems under low data constraints\n\n2026, Journal of Intelligent Manufacturing\n\n© 2025 The Authors. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].\n\nGenerally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAdaptive deep Q-networks for accurate electric vehicle range estimation (https://public-pages-files-2025.frontiersin.org/journals/big-data/articles/10.3389/fdata.2025.1697478/pdf)\nciteturn66search12 [wordlim: 200] Published: 10 months ago; Crawled: 10 months ago; <caption>TABLE 1 | Comparison of state-of-the-art techniques.</caption> ... <td>Real-world, time-stamped</td> ... <td>Range/charging demand</td> ... <td>Handles dynamic states, reduces errors</td> ... <section class="panel"><h2>Incluidos con evidencia incompleta</h2><div class="two-grid">${data.candidates.map(p=>{const f=family(p.family);return `<article class="lit-candidate">${badge(f)}<h3>${e(p.name)}</h3><p><strong>Técnica:</strong> ${e(p.model)}</p><p><strong>Documentado:</strong> ${e(p.known)}</p><p><strong>Por verificar:</strong> ${e(p.unknown)}</p><p class="caption">${e(p.evidence)}</p><div class="lit-links">${links(f)}</div></article>`;}).join('')}</div></section>
<section class="panel"><h2>Estimar presión no siempre significa pronosticarla</h2><div class="lit-negative">${data.negative_examples.map(n=>`<article><h3>${e(family(n.family).label)}</h3><p>${e(n.explanation)}</p></article>`).join('')}</div></section><section class="panel"><h2>Qué implica para nuestro objetivo a +15 minutos</h2><p>La presión futura ya existe como componente de soluciones de fugas en L-Town. Por eso “predecir presión” no basta como novedad. Los tres casos confirmados emplean un paso a +5 minutos, a menudo con presión nominal saludable como etiqueta o con escenarios modificados. Nuestro objetivo debe fijar presión <strong>observada a u+15</strong>, y medir si aporta una selección de sensores que considere relación hidráulica y antigüedad de las lecturas.</p><p>La comparación candidata incluye persistencia, regresión con rezagos y un antecedente adaptado a tres pasos. Debe reservar el test cronológico, aprender transformaciones solo en entrenamiento y evaluar MAE/RMSE por nodo, régimen y retraso. La novedad de esa combinación sigue pendiente de cerrar, especialmente frente a Tsinghua y AGN; un horizonte distinto por sí solo no demuestra un aporte nuevo.</p><p class="caption">${data.limits.map(e).join(' · ')}</p></section></div>`;}

 let selected='all';

fichas · ${rows.reduce((n,r)=>n+r.s.length,0)} registros de fuentes`;document.querySelector('#lit-results').innerHTML=rows.map(({f,s})=>`<details class="lit-family"><summary><span><strong>${e(f.label)}</strong><small>${e(goal(f.primary_goal))} · ${e(f.scope==='ltown'?'L-Town / modificación':f.scope==='other'?'Otra red':'Soporte / contexto')} · ${s.length} fuente(s)</small></span>${badge(f)}</summary><p><strong>Enfoque:</strong> ${e(f.approach)}.</p><p><strong>Aporte:</strong> ${e(f.contribution)}</p><p><strong>Decisión temporal:</strong> ${e(f.reason)}</p>${f.goals.length>1?`<p class="caption">Objetivos secundarios: ${f.goals.filter(g=>g!==f.primary_goal).map(g=>e(goal(g))).join(' · ')}</p>`:''}<div class="table-scroll"><table><thead><tr><th>Fuente</th><th>Tipo / ámbito</th><th>Evidencia y trazabilidad</th></tr></thead><tbody>${s.map(s=>`<tr><td><a href="${e(s.url)}" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">${e(s.title)} ↗</a><small>${e(s.id)} · ${e(s.year||'')}</small></td><td>${e(s.kind)}<br>${e(s.scope_description)}</td><td>${e(s.evidence)}${s.commit?`<small>Commit: ${e(s.commit)}</small>`:''}</td></tr>`).join('')}</tbody></table></div>${data.profiles.some(p=>p.family===f.id)?`<button class="secondary" data-lit-scroll="tech-${f.id}">Ver técnica temporal ↓</button>`:''}</details>`).join('')||'<p>No hay fuentes con esta combinación de filtros.</p>';};
 document.querySelectorAll('[data-lit-goal]').forEach(b=>b.onclick=()=>{selected=b.dataset.litGoal;document.querySelectorAll('[data-lit-goal]').forEach(x=>{x.classList.toggle('selected',x===b);x.setAttribute('aria-pressed',String(x===b));});draw();});
 ['lit-status','lit-scope','lit-kind','lit-search'].forEach(id=>document.getElementById(id).addEventListener('input',draw));
 document.querySelector('.literature-map').addEventListener('click',event=>{const button=event.target.closest('[data-lit-scroll]');if(button){event.preventDefault();document.getElementById(button.dataset.litScroll)?.scrollIntoView({behavior:'smooth'});}});draw();
}

# GraphConvWat -- Graph Convolution on Water Networks
GitHub repository for the paper: "Reconstructing nodal pressure in water distribution systems with graph neural networks". (under submission, preprint available at arXiv: [https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.13619](https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.13619))

## Repo structure
```
.
├── data                    - dir for data generated by generate_dta.py
├── evaluation              - scripts for evaluating and plotting the results
├── experiments             - parameters for datagen, trained model weights, etc.
├── model                   - graph neural network topologies
├── utils                   - auxiliary classes and scripts &  pip reqs.
├── water_networks          - water network topologies in EPANET-compatible format
├── generate_dta.py         - multithread scene generation
├── hyperopt.py             - hyperparameter optimization
├── LICENSE
├── README.md
├── test_Taylor_metrics.py  - calculating metrics for Taylor-diagrams
└── train.py                - training of GraphConvWat
```

# EPyT-Flow -- EPANET Python Toolkit - Flow

<img src="https://github.com/WaterFutures/EPyT-Flow/blob/main/docs/_static/net1_plot.png?raw=true" align="right" height="230px"/>

EPyT-Flow is a Python package building on top of [EPANET-PLUS](https://github.com/WaterFutures/EPANET-PLUS) --
an extension and interface of [EPANET](https://github.com/OpenWaterAnalytics/EPANET)
and [EPANET-MSX](https://github.com/OpenWaterAnalytics/epanet-msx) -- 
for providing easy access to water distribution network simulations.
It aims to provide a high-level interface for the easy generation of hydraulic and water quality scenario data.
However, it also provides access to all functions of EPANET
and EPANET-MSX.

EPyT-Flow provides easy access to popular benchmark data sets for event detection and localization.
Furthermore, it also provides an environment for developing and testing control algorithms.


## Unique Features

Unique features of EPyT-Flow that make it superior to other (Python) toolboxes are the following:

- High-performance hydraulic and (advanced) water quality simulation
- High- and low-level interface
- Object-orientated design that is easy to extend and customize
- Sensor configurations
- Wide variety of pre-defined events (e.g. leakages, sensor faults, actuator events, contamination, cyber-attacks, etc.)
- Wide variety of pre-defined types of global & local uncertainties (e.g. model uncertainties)
- Step-wise simulation and environment for training and evaluating control strategies
- Serialization module for easy exchange of data and (scenario) configurations
- REST API to make EPyT-Flow accessible in other applications
- Access to many WDNs and popular benchmarks (incl. their evaluation)

p 4 '一种城市供水管网节点压力的预测方法\n技术领域\n[0001]\n本发明属于市政工程和城市供水管网技术领域，具体涉及一种城市供水管网节点\n压力的预测方法。\n背景技术\n[0002]\n对未来城市供用水节点需水压力进行预测，可以辅助城市供水系统提前规划供水\n调度和水厂制水计划，满足未来城市用水需求。\n[0003]\n传统的做法是创建城市供水管网水力模型，对各个节点的需水量预测，将预测结\n果送入校准好的水力模型得到未来的需求压力，从而安排泵阀调度以满足未来用水需求。\n然而，创建城市供水管网水力物理模型耗时耗力，需要管网详细的GIS信息和专业的节点需\n水量/管道摩阻系数校准，带来高昂的建模成本；管网模型随着城市的发展需要不断修正，\n需要持续的维护管理成本。\n[0004]\n随着传感器的大规模部署，基于数据的数据驱动建模成为可能。数据驱动建模是\n一种基于数据分析和挖掘的建模方法，它不依赖于先验假设或理论，而是通过数据本身的\n特征和规律来构建模型。目前已有多种基于数据的方法用于城市供水时间或空间方向上的\n预测：(1)克里金插值法、反距离加权插值法等插值方法；(2)多层感知机、BP神经网络算法\n等传统机器学习方法。(3)图机器学习方法，如图切比雪夫神经网络、图卷积神经网络和其\n他图神经网络方法。插值法假定压力在管道上呈线性分布，并且空间上相似的节点具有相\n似的压力。\n[0005]\n专利文献CN113704942A公开了一种供水管网未知节点压力估计方法、装置及计算\n机系统，该方法包括：定义供水管网图拓扑结构，构建供水管网的图拉普拉斯矩阵，获取供\n水管网的压力监测点信息，基于图拉普拉斯矩阵及压力监测点信息，得到供水管网的特征\n图频率基底，获取供水管网的压力监测点在待求解时刻的压力监测值，基于压力监测值和\n特征图频率基底，计算待求解时刻供水管网的特征图频率系数，基于特征图频率系数和特\n征图频率基底，重构供水管网内各个节点的压力，以得到待求解时刻供水管网中各未知节\n点的压力值。该方法仅能对某一个特定时刻对非传感器节点压力信息进行估计，而无法对\n未来时刻进行预测。并且图拉普拉斯矩阵分解过程非常耗时，应用于大规模实际供水管网\n存在困难。\n[0006]\n专利文献CN117610826A公开了一种需水量预测方法和系统，方法包括：首先根据\n传感器得到的需水量历史监测序列进行数据的预处理，得到需水量时间序列，并经过\nProphet模型进行序列分解分析，再添加节假日因素后得到需水量天、周、月、以及趋势的预\n测图；然后，将预测值与原始序列形成的残差序列通过GRU模型，进行非线性成分的提取；最\n后将两个模型的预测值进行相加得到最终的需水量预测值。该方法缺乏对图信息的考虑，\n因此只能对布置有传感器的位置的未来需求进行时间序列预测，缺乏对非传感器位置进行\n预测的能力。且该方法没有彻底摆脱对管网水力模型的依赖。\n说\u3000明\u3000书\n1/10 页\n4\nCN 119129798 A\n4\n'
p 5 '发明内容\n[0007]\n本发明的目的在于提供一种城市供水管网节点压力的预测方法，该方法无需依赖\n水力模型，以构建用于预测供水压力数据的完全数据驱动模型。\n[0008]\n为了实现本发明的目的，提供了如下技术方案：一种城市供水管网节点压力的预\n测方法，包括以下步骤：\n[0009]\n将城市供水管网中的用水节点作为节点，以各连接的管道作为边，以构建对应的\n供水管网拓扑结构，并为供水管网拓扑结构中每条边赋予对应的管道权重，所述用水节点\n以位置信息和是否设有传感器进行标签标注；\n[0010]\n获取城市供水管网中各传感器的节点压力数据，并基于预设时间步长对节点压力\n数据进行截取，以获取对应的压力测量值，将所述压力测量值与用水节点的标签组成数据\n集；\n[0011]\n基于transformer模型框架和供水管网拓扑结构构建预测模型，所述预测模型包\n括特征提取模块，融合模块，注意力操作模块和预测模块：\n[0012]\n所述特征提取模块包括时间提取部分和空间提取部分，所述时间提取部分基于压\n力数据采样时间点，生成对应的时间嵌入向量，所述空间提取部分基于所述供水管网拓扑\n结构各节点之间的连接关系，获得对应节点的连接性嵌入向量；\n[0013]\n所述融合模块，用于将连接性嵌入向量与供水管网拓扑结构中各节点的标签进行\n拼接，以获得对应节点的空间嵌入向量，并将所述空间嵌入向量与所述时间嵌入向量进行\n拼接，以输出时空嵌入向量；\n[0014]\n所述注意力操作模块，用于对输入的时空嵌入向量进行时间注意力操作和空间注\n意力操作，以获得对应的空间注意力分数和空间注意力分数并进行加权计算，输出最终注\n意力得分；\n[0015]\n所述预测模块，根据输入的最终注意力得分进行预测，以输出预测结果；\n[0016]\n利用数据集对预测模型进行训练，以获得用于预测无传感器用水节点压力的节点\n压力预测模型；\n[0017]\n将目标用水节点的位置信息和预测时间点输入至节点压力预测模型，以获得目标\n用水节点的节点压力数据。\n[0018]\n本发明将传感器数据采样时间和传感器在管网中的位置抽象成时空嵌入向量，采\n用注意力机制分别对时空嵌入向量提取节点的时空特征，从而通过数据驱动模型，将布置\n有传感器节点的压力信息泛化到缺少传感器的节点，以实现最终节点压力数据的预测。\n[0019]\n具体的，所述管道权重通过海增‑威廉公式进行近似计算获得。\n[0020]\n具体的，所述时间提取部分以管网压力变化的周次序和日次序，分别用独热编码\n表示后通过拼接获得。\n[0021]\n具体的，所述空间提取部分采用Node2Vec算法对供水管网拓扑结构进行分析，以\n获得对应的连接性嵌入向量。\n[0022]\n具体的，所述融合模块中的拼接表达式如下：\n[0023]\n[0024]\n其中，\n表示m维的向量，\n表示n维的向量。\n[0025]\n具体的，所述时间注意力操作通过固定时刻嵌入信息，对第l层消息传递过程，时\n说\u3000明\u3000书\n2/10 页\n5\nCN 119129798 A\n5\n'

p 7 '空上的定位；通过对不同时空传感器采集的压力值进行随机掩码(随机数量和随机位置)，\n增强模型对未遇见数据的泛化预测能力。\n附图说明\n[0036]\n图1为本实施例提供的城市供水管网节点压力的预测方法的流程图；\n[0037]\n图2为本实施例提供的供水管网拓扑结构的示意图；\n[0038]\n图3为本实施例提供的预测模型的框架示意图；\n[0039]\n图4为本实施例提供的在预测步长为12的情况下的时空预测效果图。\n具体实施方式\n[0040]\n为使本发明实施例的目的、技术方案和优点更加清楚，下面将结合本发明实施例\n中附图，对本发明实施例中的技术方案进行清楚、完整地描述，显然，所描述的实施例仅仅\n是本发明一部分实施例，而不是全部的实施例。通常在此处附图中描述和示出的本发明实\n施例的组件可以以各种不同的配置来布置和设计。因此，以下对在附图中提供的本发明的\n实施例的详细描述并非旨在限制要求保护的本发明的范围，而是仅仅表示本发明的选定实\n施例。基于本发明中的实施例，本领域普通技术人员在没有做出创造性劳动前提下所获得\n的所有其他实施例，都属于本发明保护的范围。\n[0041]\n如图1所示，为本实施例提供的一种城市供水管网节点压力的预测方法，包括以下\n步骤：\n[0042]\n定义供水管网图拓扑结构：将供水管网中的用水节点视为图论中的节点，管道视\n为图论中的边，使用图论理论描述供水管网拓扑结构，使用海增‑威廉姆斯公式近似描述管\n道权重，为后续Node2Vec图嵌入方法提供基础。\n[0043]\n定义供水管网图拓扑结构的表征方式：将压力监测点在供水管网中的位置信息嵌\n入成空间向量；将压力监测点采样时间嵌入成时间向量。\n[0044]\n图矩阵表征方式的维度已成为限制其在实际管网上应用的主要障碍。基于随机游\n走的图表征方式能够在保留局部和全局网络结构的基础上，大大降低网络拓扑表示法的维\n度，可作为高效的图表征方式。由于用水的规律性，管网中压力呈现周期性变化趋势。因此，\n将这种周期性纳入预测考量将有助于提高预测的准确性。\n[0045]\n定义供水管网图拓扑结构的学习方式：基于时空向量，构建时空向量注意力机制，\n将传感器节点信息迁移到缺乏传感器节点，得到全局未来节点信息。\n[0046]\n在供水管网中利用注意力机制一种有效的方法，可以自适应地捕捉节点间的相关\n关系，自适应的捕捉节点之间的长程依赖关。通过为不同位置分配不同权重，从而动态地将\n多个已知传感器节点的信息融合到目标非传感器节点\n[0047]\n在本实例中，其具体构建过程如下：\n[0048]\n构建城市供水管网拓扑有权无向图，其中管网的用水节点被表示成图的节点，管\n网管段被表示成图的边：拓扑图包含供水管网中管道的连接情况和连接管道的权重系数，\n用用水节点可分为布置有压力传感器节点和没有布置压力传感器的节点。\n[0049]\n其中，拓扑有权无向图，由三部分构成：起始节点、终止节点、连接权重。\n[0050]\n连接的权重系数\n通过海增‑威廉公式进行近似计算，近似过程如下：\n说\u3000明\u3000书\n4/10 页\n7\nCN 119129798 A\n7\n'
p 8 '[0051]\n[0052]\n最终构建的供水管网拓扑结构，如图2所示。\n[0053]\n获取城市供水管网中各传感器的节点压力数据，并基于预设时间步长对节点压力\n数据进行截取，以获取对应的压力测量值，将所述压力测量值与用水节点的标签组成数据\n集。\n[0054]\n如图3所示，基于transformer模型框架和供水管网拓扑结构构建预测模型，所述\n预测模型包括特征提取模块，融合模块，注意力操作模块和预测模块：\n[0055]\n所述特征提取模块包括时间提取部分和空间提取部分，所述时间提取部分基于压\n力数据采样时间点，生成对应的时间嵌入向量，所述空间提取部分基于所述供水管网拓扑\n结构各节点之间的连接关系，获得对应节点的连接性嵌入向量；\n[0056]\n所述融合模块，用于将连接性嵌入向量与供水管网拓扑结构中各节点的标签进行\n拼接，以获得对应节点的空间嵌入向量，并将所述空间嵌入向量与所述时间嵌入向量进行\n拼接，以输出时空嵌入向量；\n[0057]\n所述注意力操作模块，用于对输入的时空嵌入向量进行时间注意力操作和空间注\n意力操作，以获得对应的空间注意力分数和空间注意力分数并进行加权计算，输出最终注\n意力得分；\n[0058]\n所述预测模块，根据输入的最终注意力得分进行预测，以输出预测结果。\n[0059]\n更进一步地，特征提取模块中将压力监测点在供水管网中的位置信息嵌入成空间\n向量；将压力监测点采样时间嵌入成时间向量；\n[0060]\n根据传感器采样时间点生成时间嵌入向量：将传感器采样时间表征为周次序和日\n次序双周期，分别用独热编码表示并拼接在一起，其中时间表征为周次序和日次序双周期，\n分别用独热编码表示并拼接在一起\n[0061]\n其中周次序，即传感器采样时间在星期几；\n[0062]\n而日次序，即传感器采样时间在一天中的第几次采样\n[0063]\n而在空间提取部分根据Node2Vec算法依据拓扑有权无向图生成管网每个用用水\n节点的空间嵌入向量：Node2Vec是一种用于图表示学习的算法框架，目标是为图中的节点\n学习连续的特征表示用于各种下游机器学习任务，根据图信息生成空间向量嵌入包含的过\n程如下：\n[0064]\n从节点到节点生成训练样本。首先，从每个节点开始进行特定次数的有偏游走。有\n偏游走依据管道权重从一个节点到下一个节点，权重大的管道被选择为下一个游走方向的\n概率也更大。\n[0065]\n将生成的随机游走节点序列视为文本语料，使用SkipGram模型学习节点的特征向\n量，使用负采样加速训练过程。具体的，对于序列中的每一个节点，SkipGram模型试图使用\n说\u3000明\u3000书\n5/10 页\n8\nCN 119129798 A\n8\n'

p 8 8 GANs trained in a semi-supervised manner have shown great promise, even with few labeled data 183 (Mu et al., 2021). The GAN architecture, in its basic form, is composed of a generator (𝐺) and a 184 discriminator (𝐷) neural network (Goodfellow et al., 2014). A random input latent space 𝑧~𝑝𝑧 185 (often sampled from a normal or uniform probability distribution, e.g. 𝑧~𝒩(0,1)) is mapped to 186 the data space 𝜙 by the generator, which tries to generate new examples that are ideally identical 187 to the training dataset 𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎. To simulate the leaks at random pipes across the network, the calibrated model first randomly selects the nodes available connected to defined pipe IDs. Upon targeting the random nodes selected, the leak condition is then simulated at the respective connected pipes using the time boundary conditions from Equations (2a – 2c) using a leak percent of 10% of the corresponding calibrated pipe diameter size. Each simulation run will be performed for the period of 365 days. For extensiveness, the following quantities of the anomaly nodes are simulated: (a) 50 to 100; (b) 100 to 200; (c) 200 to 300; (d) 300 to 400; and (e) 400 to 500. In each scenario, a total of 200 simulation runs has been performed which generally incurred an average computational time of 6 hours due to multiple iterations involved to handle the relatively vast number of anomalies occurring at the different node locations. 100 simulation sets are then randomly picked from each scenario to build a common pool of simulation data which is subsequently used for training and validating a 1D feedforward DNN model for classification analysis. FAMILY li-chen
p 8 Water 2026, 18, 2220 8 of 28 was constructed to systematically evaluate the performance differences among various modeling strategies in the classification task. 2.3.1. Random Forest RF is an ensemble learning method that constructs a strong learner by combining multiple decision trees. This method employs bootstrap sampling to generate multiple training subsets and introduces a random feature selection mechanism during the tree construction process, thereby effectively reducing the correlation among individual models. By aggregating the prediction results of all decision trees, RF can improve model robustness and generalization ability while maintaining high prediction accuracy [37]. 2.3.2. Gradient Boosting Decision Tree (GBDT) Gradient boosting decision tree (GBDT) models build an ensemble of decision trees sequentially, with each newly added tree trained to correct the residual errors of the existing ensemble. In this study, XGBoost and LightGBM, two representative GBDT-based algorithms, were adopted. Although both algorithms follow the same additive boosting framework, XGBoost improves predictive performance through regularized objective optimization and exact or approximate split finding, whereas LightGBM emphasizes computational efficiency by employing histogram-based feature discretization together with leaf-wise tree growth. Consequently, the final prediction is generated by aggregating the outputs of multiple decision trees [38,39]: byi,GBDT = K ∑ k=1 fk(xi) (5) where xi denotes the input feature vector of the i-th sample, K represents the number of decision trees
p 9 Water 2026, 18, 2220 9 of 28 Figure 2. Framework of voting. 2.3.5. Stacking Stacking is a hierarchical ensemble learning method whose fundamental idea is to improve overall predictive performance by constructing a multi-level model structure. In this method, multiple base models are first trained independently on the original training data to generate corresponding prediction outputs. Subsequently, the prediction results of the base models are used as new input features for a meta-model, which performs further learning to optimize the final prediction results. Compared with single models, Stacking can effectively integrate the representational capabilities of different models, reduce generalization error, and improve prediction stability and accuracy. Therefore, it has strong application value in complex tasks. Figure 3 presents a schematic illustration of this algorithm [42]. Figure 3. Framework of stacking. https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220

Using Python, the data set was transformed into a binary matrix where 0’s and 1’s represented normal and anomalous values respectively from the sensors in the network. These anomalous values are the statistical outliers. i.e., values beyond 1.5 times the Inter-Quartile Range.
p 3 Page | 3 The dimensions of the binary matrix were then reduced by discarding rows that contained only normal readings – resulting in a binary matrix with one or many anomalous records in each row. We termed this matrix as “Anomaly Matrix”, which was the basis of detection and localization of leakage instances. A Checksum was introduced to compute the sum of each row. This Checksum would signify the number of simultaneous anomaly occurrences in one or multiple sensor nodes at Tnth instance. Anomalous data points that occurred at regular/continuous time intervals were identified. This interval was calibrated by analyzing the patterns from the known leaked pipes from the 2018 Leak Report. In the following illustration (Figure 7), Timestamp T9 is also considered because on T9th instance, there were multiple (two) anomalous readings, and it also is in succession of previously recorded anomaly timestamps T6, T7 and T8.

Random Forest RF is an ensemble learning method that constructs a strong learner by combining multiple decision trees. This method employs bootstrap sampling to generate multiple training subsets and introduces a random feature selection mechanism during the tree construction process, thereby effectively reducing the correlation among individual models. By aggregating the prediction results of all decision trees, RF can improve model robustness and generalization ability while maintaining high prediction accuracy [37]. 2.3.2. Gradient Boosting Decision Tree (GBDT) Gradient boosting decision tree (GBDT) models build an ensemble of decision trees sequentially, with each newly added tree trained to correct the residual errors of the existing ensemble. In this study, XGBoost and LightGBM, two representative GBDT-based algorithms, were adopted. Although both algorithms follow the same additive boosting framework, XGBoost improves predictive performance through regularized objective optimization and exact or approximate split finding, whereas LightGBM emphasizes computational efficiency by employing histogram-based feature discretization together with leaf-wise tree growth. Consequently, the final prediction is generated by aggregating the outputs of multiple decision trees [38,39]: byi,GBDT = K ∑ k=1 fk(xi) (5) where xi denotes the input feature vector of the i-th sample, K represents the number of decision trees\\r\\np 9 Water 2026, 18, 2220 9 of 28 Figure 2. Framework of voting. 2.3.5. Stacking Stacking is a hierarchical ensemble learning method whose fundamental idea is to improve overall predictive performance by constructing a multi-level model structure. In this method, multiple base models are first trained independently on the original training data to generate corresponding prediction outputs. Subsequently, the prediction results of the base models are used as new input features for a meta-model, which performs further learning to optimize the final prediction results. Compared with single models, Stacking can effectively integrate the representational capabilities of different models, reduce generalization error, and improve prediction stability and accuracy. Therefore, it has strong application value in complex tasks. Figure 3 presents a schematic illustration of this algorithm [42]. Figure 3. Forecasting with different prediction window sizes., Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t179 container abstracts\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t180 container abs0001\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t181 heading Highlights, Value: 2\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t182 text Highlights\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t183 content list\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t184 container\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t185 text •\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t186 text Attention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t187 container\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t188 text •\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t189 text Model is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t190 container\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t191 text •\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t192 text Model exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t193 container\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t194 text •\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t195 text Model explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t196 container abs0002\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t197 heading Abstract, Value: 2\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t198 text Abstract\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t199 text Efficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t200 container abs0003\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t201 heading Graphical abstract, Value: 2\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t202 text Graphical abstract\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t203 container fig0008\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t204 image Image, graphical abstract\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t205 content list\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t206 link Description: Download: Download high-res image (273KB), Help: Download high-res image (273KB), Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t207 link Description: Download: Download full-size image, Help: Download full-size image, Value: ars.els-cdn.com/con…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t208 container keys0001\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t209 heading Keywords, Value: 2\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t210 text Keywords\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t211 text Explainability\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t212 text ; \\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t213 text Real-time\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t214 text ; \\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t215 text Demand forecasting\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t216 text ; \\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t217 text Attention mechanisms\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t218 text ; \\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t219 text Graph neural network\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t220 text ; \\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t221 text Water distribution system\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t222 content list\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t223 link Description: Previous article, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t224 link Description: Next article, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t225 container body\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t226 container sec0001\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t227 heading 1. Introduction, Value: 2, ID: cesectitle0006\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t228 text 1 .  Introduction\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t229 container para0006\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t230 text As urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t231 link Description: 10, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t232 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t233 link Description: 25, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t234 text ]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t235 link Description: 11, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t236 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t237 link Description: 35, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t238 text ]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t239 link Description: 26, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t240 text ]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t241 link Description: 12, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t242 text ].\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t243 container para0007\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t244 text Generally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t245 link Description: 4, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t246 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t247 link Description: 5, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t248 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t249 link Description: 9, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t250 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t251 link Description: 15, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t252 text ]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t253 link Description: 8, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t254 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t255 link Description: 27, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t256 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t257 link Description: 36, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t258 text ], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t259 link Description: 23, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t260 text ]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t261 link Description: 1, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t262 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t263 link Description: 33, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t264 text ]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Consequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t265 container para0008\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t266 text Several studies [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t267 link Description: 18, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t268 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t269 link Description: 33, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t270 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t271 link Description: 37, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t272 text ] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t273 link Description: 37, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t274 text ] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t275 link Description: 33, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t276 text ] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t277 link Description: 18, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t278 text ] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t279 container para0009\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t280 text Despite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t281 link Description: 16, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t282 text ]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t283 link Description: 29, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t284 text ]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t285 link Description: 13, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t286 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t287 link Description: 21, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t288 text ], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t289 link Description: 22, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t290 text ]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t291 container para0010\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t292 text The literature [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t293 link Description: 34, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t294 text ] defines a classification standard for interpretability methods within the GNN framework. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t295 link Description: 14, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t296 text ,\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t297 link Description: 28, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t298 text ]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t299 link Description: 20, Value: sciencedirect.com/s…\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t300 text ].\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t301 text This study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t302 text ·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t303 text Model explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t304 container sec0002\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t305 heading 2. Methodology, Value: 2, ID: cesectitle0007\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t306 text .  Methodology\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t307 text This study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t308 container sec0003\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t\\t309 heading 2.1.
